  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22, F06.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because people keep telling me I’m making too much of things, but I’m not. I know when I’m being watched. The hospital people, and some people from the agency, they’ve been watching our house and even my cleaning routes, like checking up on me to see if I’m taking proper care of my husband.\n\nAnd I do take care of him, you know. I’ve always been a hard worker, I clean the way my mother taught me, corners and baseboards, not just the middle where people look. But lately it feels like everywhere I go, somebody is looking, like they’re keeping notes. That’s why I came in, because everyone acts like I’m imagining it, and I need someone to understand what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, sometimes. Not all the time. When the house is quiet, especially by the kitchen, I’ll hear a woman’s voice near the window, clear like she’s right there. She tells me to check the locks, or move his pills.\n\nAnd I do check the locks anyway, because you can’t be too careful—my mother always said a house should be kept proper, doors, windows, everything in its place. But the voice, it’s not like a thought exactly. It sounds outside of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any major medical illness or head injury before these experiences started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not that I can point to. I’ve had the usual aches from cleaning houses, you know, knees and back, carrying buckets and bending all day, but a major illness or hitting my head bad before all this? I don’t think so.\n\nUnless you mean something like when you get worn down and tired—because people don’t count that, but it does something to you. My mother used to say you can scrub a floor until it shines and still nobody sees what it costs you. But no, I can’t think of a head injury or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sister says that sometimes. She says, “Slow down, I don’t know what you’re talking about.” But I am talking about the same thing, it’s just all connected.\n\nLike when I try to explain about my husband’s heart pills, then I remember the church schedule because that’s when someone could come by, and then the numbers on my client list matter too, because the order of them—well, people act like I skipped something. They say they can’t follow me, but I know what I mean. It just comes out tangled, I guess.\n\nDoctor: For longer than one month, have you been having these experiences of being watched or hearing the woman’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than a month. It’s been, I’d say, around eleven months now with the woman’s voice, and that feeling like people are watching us.\n\nIt started kind of small, like you think maybe it’s just the neighbors or the curtains not sitting right—my mother was always fussy about curtains, said people could see in if you didn’t hang them proper—but no, it’s been nearly a year. Not every minute, but on and off for about eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you been less able to manage your usual work or daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be the one people called when a house was a mess and they needed it done right. But the agency, they took away my supervisory routes. They said clients were complaining, and some of the girls I worked with said they couldn’t count on me the same way anymore.\n\nAnd at home it’s been worse too. My husband needs things kept in order, and I always kept the house running, you know—laundry, meals, appointments, all the little things nobody sees. But lately my husband and my pastor have had to step in more, because I’m not keeping up safely like I used to. It makes me feel ashamed, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you become much less interested in being with other people or doing things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I still care about people… it’s not like I stopped caring. Sometimes I don’t feel like being around a lot of chatter, but then again, some people talk over you and don’t listen anyway.\n\nI don’t know if that’s “less interested” or just me being tired of explaining myself. What do you mean by things I used to care about?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your emotions feel flatter than they used to—for example, that your face shows less expression or your feelings seem muted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… flatter, like my face? I haven’t really been looking at myself that way. My husband might say I look tired, but he says that when I don’t put my lipstick on too, and honestly lipstick never sits right after a long cleaning shift anyway.\n\nI still feel things, I think. I get upset, I get embarrassed… sometimes I laugh if something’s actually funny. But muted? I’m not sure. Maybe I’m just worn down. What do you mean by “flatter,” like not crying, or just not showing it on my face?\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have people told you that you seem less expressive in your face or voice than before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it exactly like that… “less expressive.” My husband will say, “What’s that look for?” even when I’m not trying to have any look, but he says things like that when he’s irritated too.\n\nAnd my pastor, he’s more gentle, he’ll ask if I’m tired or if I need to sit a minute. But I don’t know if that means my face is different. My voice… I mean, after cleaning all day, anybody’s voice gets low, especially if you’ve been around bleach and dust. I had one client once who kept all these little glass birds on shelves, and dusting around them made me so nervous because if one broke—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. Nobody has really told me plainly that my face or voice is less expressive.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it much harder to get yourself started on everyday tasks, even when you want to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Some days I do drag my feet, but then, doesn’t everybody? If I know the laundry needs doing or the dishes are sitting there, I’ll usually get to it… maybe not right away.\n\nGetting started is funny, because once I’m moving I can keep going, especially with cleaning. At work you don’t really get to sit around wondering if you feel like it—the sink is dirty, the floor needs mopping, you do it. At home it’s different, because the little things pile up, and then I start thinking about which one to do first, and then the phone rings or I remember I need to check if we’re out of trash bags.\n\nSo… harder? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s “much harder.” What kind of tasks do you mean?\n\nDoctor: During the times when you hear the woman’s voice or feel watched, have you also been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day. Sometimes I’m scared or worn out when that happens, and then the whole day feels kind of… heavy, I guess. But I don’t always stop and separate out, like, “Is this sadness, or is this nerves, or am I just tired?”\n\nLike yesterday I was folding towels and I kept thinking I heard something, and then I remembered I had left a rag in the bucket, which is the worst because it gets that sour smell, and then I got upset with myself. So was I depressed? I don’t know. I know I don’t feel right during those times, but “depressed most of the day” is hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your behavior seems strange or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… my husband has said I’m acting “weird,” mostly when I’m going back through the rooms after I already cleaned them. Like I’ll line up the spray bottles under the sink, or the towels have to sit a certain way, and if a chair is angled wrong I’ll fix it and then come back and check it again.\n\nAt work too, sometimes I catch myself arranging things in little patterns—bottles, towels, chairs—and walking room to room again because it just feels wrong to leave it otherwise. People don’t always say it straight out, but I can tell they don’t understand it. And then I start thinking about how some people don’t even rinse the sink after toothpaste, which is—sorry, that’s not what you asked. So yes, I guess they have said it seems strange sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as if someone else is putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Putting thoughts in… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. It’s more that I’ll get a feeling, or I’ll notice something, and then people act like I’m making it up.\n\nThe reason I’m here is because everyone keeps saying I’m imagining things, but I know the hospital people and some people from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. They want to see if I’m taking proper care of my husband. And it’s not like I just decided that out of nowhere—there are little things, like timing, and who shows up, and what they seem to know.\n\nSo no, not exactly putting thoughts in my head. More like they’re watching, and then I can’t stop thinking about what they’re checking.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems begin suddenly, within a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not really within a couple weeks. It’s been… longer than that. I’d say this whole change has been going on about a year and a couple months, since after my husband was in the hospital for his heart.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me trying to keep everything together after that, because when someone comes home from the hospital there’s all these papers and instructions and you’re looking at the floors and the bathroom and thinking everything has to be right. But it didn’t just pass in a week or two. It’s been more like… around a year and two months now.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a neurological illness, such as dementia, Parkinson’s disease, epilepsy, or a stroke?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has told me that, no. I mean, I’ve been to doctors for regular things, and they ask all these questions and put things in the chart, but I don’t remember anyone saying I had something like that.\n\nMy husband had all the serious hospital stuff, so sometimes I get mixed up about which doctor said what to who, because there were so many papers and appointments… but for me, no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: When you talk, do people often tell you that you jump from one topic to another in a way they can’t follow?\n\nPatient: Yes… people do say that. Mostly when I’m trying to explain what’s been happening, because it all connects in my head, but when I say it out loud it comes out wrong.\n\nLike I’ll start with my husband’s heart pills, because that’s where I notice things, and then I’m talking about the church schedule, and then the numbers on my client list, and my sister will say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” or “You’re jumping around.” But I’m not trying to confuse anybody. I’m trying to get all the pieces in the right order, and they don’t let me finish.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences began, had you started using any new medication or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so, but then there are always little things—vitamins, something for a headache, whatever someone tells you is good at church or at the store. I try to be careful because after my husband’s hospital stay there were so many bottles around, and I was always checking labels and moving things so they wouldn’t get mixed up.\n\nBut for me, like a new drug or something I started right before all this… I can’t really say there was one. I don’t use street drugs, if that’s what you mean. Medications, I’d have to look at the bottles or the papers because I don’t want to say it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have people noticed that you show much less emotion in your face or voice than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really noticed my face, unless I’m looking in the mirror, and who really does that except when you’re fixing your hair or checking if you look tired before work.\n\nPeople have told me I look tired or serious sometimes, but that could be anything. What do you mean by “flatter,” like not crying, or just not showing it on my face?\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have people told you that you seem less expressive in your face or voice than before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it exactly like that… “less expressive.” My husband will say, “What’s that look for?” even when I’m not trying to have any look, but he says things like that when he’s irritated too.\n\nAnd my pastor, he’s more gentle, he’ll ask if I’m tired or if I need to sit a minute. But I don’t know if that means my face is different. My voice… I mean, after cleaning all day, anybody’s voice gets low, especially if you’ve been around bleach and dust. I had one client once who kept all these little glass birds on shelves, and dusting around them made me so nervous because if one broke—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. Nobody has really told me plainly that my face or voice is less expressive.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it much harder to get yourself started on everyday tasks, even when you want to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Some days I do drag my feet, but then, doesn’t everybody? If I know the laundry needs doing or the dishes are sitting there, I’ll usually get to it… maybe not right away.\n\nGetting started is funny, because once I’m moving I can keep going, especially with cleaning. At work you don’t really get to sit around wondering if you feel like it—the sink is dirty, the floor needs mopping, you do it. At home it’s different, because the little things pile up, and then I start thinking about which one to do first, and then the phone rings or I remember I need to check if we’re out of trash bags.\n\nSo… harder? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s “much harder.” What kind of tasks do you mean?\n\nDoctor: During the times when you hear the woman’s voice or feel watched, have you also been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day. Sometimes I’m scared or worn out when that happens, and then the whole day feels kind of… heavy, I guess. But I don’t always stop and separate out, like, “Is this sadness, or is this nerves, or am I just tired?”\n\nLike yesterday I was folding towels and I kept thinking I heard something, and then I remembered I had left a rag in the bucket, which is the worst because it gets that sour smell, and then I got upset with myself. So was I depressed? I don’t know. I know I don’t feel right during those times, but “depressed most of the day” is hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your behavior seems strange or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… my husband has said I’m acting “weird,” mostly when I’m going back through the rooms after I already cleaned them. Like I’ll line up the spray bottles under the sink, or the towels have to sit a certain way, and if a chair is angled wrong I’ll fix it and then come back and check it again.\n\nAt work too, sometimes I catch myself arranging things in little patterns—bottles, towels, chairs—and walking room to room again because it just feels wrong to leave it otherwise. People don’t always say it straight out, but I can tell they don’t understand it. And then I start thinking about how some people don’t even rinse the sink after toothpaste, which is—sorry, that’s not what you asked. So yes, I guess they have said it seems strange sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as if someone else is putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Putting thoughts in… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. It’s more that I’ll get a feeling, or I’ll notice something, and then people act like I’m making it up.\n\nThe reason I’m here is because everyone keeps saying I’m imagining things, but I know the hospital people and some people from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. They want to see if I’m taking proper care of my husband. And it’s not like I just decided that out of nowhere—there are little things, like timing, and who shows up, and what they seem to know.\n\nSo no, not exactly putting thoughts in my head. More like they’re watching, and then I can’t stop thinking about what they’re checking.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems begin suddenly, within a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not really within a couple weeks. It’s been… longer than that. I’d say this whole change has been going on about a year and a couple months, since after my husband was in the hospital for his heart.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me trying to keep everything together after that, because when someone comes home from the hospital there’s all these papers and instructions and you’re looking at the floors and the bathroom and thinking everything has to be right. But it didn’t just pass in a week or two. It’s been more like… around a year and two months now.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a neurological illness, such as dementia, Parkinson’s disease, epilepsy, or a stroke?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has told me that, no. I mean, I’ve been to doctors for regular things, and they ask all these questions and put things in the chart, but I don’t remember anyone saying I had something like that.\n\nMy husband had all the serious hospital stuff, so sometimes I get mixed up about which doctor said what to who, because there were so many papers and appointments… but for me, no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: When you talk, do people often tell you that you jump from one topic to another in a way they can’t follow?\n\nPatient: Yes… people do say that. Mostly when I’m trying to explain what’s been happening, because it all connects in my head, but when I say it out loud it comes out wrong.\n\nLike I’ll start with my husband’s heart pills, because that’s where I notice things, and then I’m talking about the church schedule, and then the numbers on my client list, and my sister will say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” or “You’re jumping around.” But I’m not trying to confuse anybody. I’m trying to get all the pieces in the right order, and they don’t let me finish.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences began, had you started using any new medication or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so, but then there are always little things—vitamins, something for a headache, whatever someone tells you is good at church or at the store. I try to be careful because after my husband’s hospital stay there were so many bottles around, and I was always checking labels and moving things so they wouldn’t get mixed up.\n\nBut for me, like a new drug or something I started right before all this… I can’t really say there was one. I don’t use street drugs, if that’s what you mean. Medications, I’d have to look at the bottles or the papers because I don’t want to say it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have people noticed that you show much less emotion in your face or voice than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really noticed my face, unless I’m looking in the mirror, and who really does that except when you’re fixing your hair or checking if you look tired before work.\n\nPeople have told me I look tired or serious sometimes, but that could be anything. When you’re cleaning houses all day and then somebody wants to talk and talk, you don’t always have a big expression ready, you know? My sister might say, “Why are you looking like that?” but she says that about everybody.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s less emotion, exactly. I don’t think anyone has said it that way to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22, F06.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because people keep telling me I’m making too much of things, but I’m not. I know when I’m being watched. The hospital people, and some people from the agency, they’ve been watching our house and even my cleaning routes, like checking up on me to see if I’m taking proper care of my husband.\n\nAnd I do take care of him, you know. I’ve always been a hard worker, I clean the way my mother taught me, corners and baseboards, not just the middle where people look. But lately it feels like everywhere I go, somebody is looking, like they’re keeping notes. That’s why I came in, because everyone acts like I’m imagining it, and I need someone to understand what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, sometimes. Not all the time. When the house is quiet, especially by the kitchen, I’ll hear a woman’s voice near the window, clear like she’s right there. She tells me to check the locks, or move his pills.\n\nAnd I do check the locks anyway, because you can’t be too careful—my mother always said a house should be kept proper, doors, windows, everything in its place. But the voice, it’s not like a thought exactly. It sounds outside of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any major medical illness or head injury before these experiences started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not that I can point to. I’ve had the usual aches from cleaning houses, you know, knees and back, carrying buckets and bending all day, but a major illness or hitting my head bad before all this? I don’t think so.\n\nUnless you mean something like when you get worn down and tired—because people don’t count that, but it does something to you. My mother used to say you can scrub a floor until it shines and still nobody sees what it costs you. But no, I can’t think of a head injury or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sister says that sometimes. She says, “Slow down, I don’t know what you’re talking about.” But I am talking about the same thing, it’s just all connected.\n\nLike when I try to explain about my husband’s heart pills, then I remember the church schedule because that’s when someone could come by, and then the numbers on my client list matter too, because the order of them—well, people act like I skipped something. They say they can’t follow me, but I know what I mean. It just comes out tangled, I guess.\n\nDoctor: For longer than one month, have you been having these experiences of being watched or hearing the woman’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than a month. It’s been, I’d say, around eleven months now with the woman’s voice, and that feeling like people are watching us.\n\nIt started kind of small, like you think maybe it’s just the neighbors or the curtains not sitting right—my mother was always fussy about curtains, said people could see in if you didn’t hang them proper—but no, it’s been nearly a year. Not every minute, but on and off for about eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you been less able to manage your usual work or daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be the one people called when a house was a mess and they needed it done right. But the agency, they took away my supervisory routes. They said clients were complaining, and some of the girls I worked with said they couldn’t count on me the same way anymore.\n\nAnd at home it’s been worse too. My husband needs things kept in order, and I always kept the house running, you know—laundry, meals, appointments, all the little things nobody sees. But lately my husband and my pastor have had to step in more, because I’m not keeping up safely like I used to. It makes me feel ashamed, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you become much less interested in being with other people or doing things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I still care about people… it’s not like I stopped caring. Sometimes I don’t feel like being around a lot of chatter, but then again, some people talk over you and don’t listen anyway.\n\nI don’t know if that’s “less interested” or just me being tired of explaining myself. What do you mean by things I used to care about?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your emotions feel flatter than they used to—for example, that your face shows less expression or your feelings seem muted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… flatter, like my face? I haven’t really been looking at myself that way. My husband might say I look tired, but he says that when I don’t put my lipstick on too, and honestly lipstick never sits right after a long cleaning shift anyway.\n\nI still feel things, I think. I get upset, I get embarrassed… sometimes I laugh if something’s actually funny. But muted? I’m not sure. Maybe I’m just worn down. What do you mean by “flatter,” like not crying, or just not showing it on my face?\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have people told you that you seem less expressive in your face or voice than before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it exactly like that… “less expressive.” My husband will say, “What’s that look for?” even when I’m not trying to have any look, but he says things like that when he’s irritated too.\n\nAnd my pastor, he’s more gentle, he’ll ask if I’m tired or if I need to sit a minute. But I don’t know if that means my face is different. My voice… I mean, after cleaning all day, anybody’s voice gets low, especially if you’ve been around bleach and dust. I had one client once who kept all these little glass birds on shelves, and dusting around them made me so nervous because if one broke—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. Nobody has really told me plainly that my face or voice is less expressive.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it much harder to get yourself started on everyday tasks, even when you want to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Some days I do drag my feet, but then, doesn’t everybody? If I know the laundry needs doing or the dishes are sitting there, I’ll usually get to it… maybe not right away.\n\nGetting started is funny, because once I’m moving I can keep going, especially with cleaning. At work you don’t really get to sit around wondering if you feel like it—the sink is dirty, the floor needs mopping, you do it. At home it’s different, because the little things pile up, and then I start thinking about which one to do first, and then the phone rings or I remember I need to check if we’re out of trash bags.\n\nSo… harder? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s “much harder.” What kind of tasks do you mean?\n\nDoctor: During the times when you hear the woman’s voice or feel watched, have you also been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day. Sometimes I’m scared or worn out when that happens, and then the whole day feels kind of… heavy, I guess. But I don’t always stop and separate out, like, “Is this sadness, or is this nerves, or am I just tired?”\n\nLike yesterday I was folding towels and I kept thinking I heard something, and then I remembered I had left a rag in the bucket, which is the worst because it gets that sour smell, and then I got upset with myself. So was I depressed? I don’t know. I know I don’t feel right during those times, but “depressed most of the day” is hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your behavior seems strange or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… my husband has said I’m acting “weird,” mostly when I’m going back through the rooms after I already cleaned them. Like I’ll line up the spray bottles under the sink, or the towels have to sit a certain way, and if a chair is angled wrong I’ll fix it and then come back and check it again.\n\nAt work too, sometimes I catch myself arranging things in little patterns—bottles, towels, chairs—and walking room to room again because it just feels wrong to leave it otherwise. People don’t always say it straight out, but I can tell they don’t understand it. And then I start thinking about how some people don’t even rinse the sink after toothpaste, which is—sorry, that’s not what you asked. So yes, I guess they have said it seems strange sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as if someone else is putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Putting thoughts in… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. It’s more that I’ll get a feeling, or I’ll notice something, and then people act like I’m making it up.\n\nThe reason I’m here is because everyone keeps saying I’m imagining things, but I know the hospital people and some people from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. They want to see if I’m taking proper care of my husband. And it’s not like I just decided that out of nowhere—there are little things, like timing, and who shows up, and what they seem to know.\n\nSo no, not exactly putting thoughts in my head. More like they’re watching, and then I can’t stop thinking about what they’re checking.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems begin suddenly, within a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not really within a couple weeks. It’s been… longer than that. I’d say this whole change has been going on about a year and a couple months, since after my husband was in the hospital for his heart.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me trying to keep everything together after that, because when someone comes home from the hospital there’s all these papers and instructions and you’re looking at the floors and the bathroom and thinking everything has to be right. But it didn’t just pass in a week or two. It’s been more like… around a year and two months now.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a neurological illness, such as dementia, Parkinson’s disease, epilepsy, or a stroke?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has told me that, no. I mean, I’ve been to doctors for regular things, and they ask all these questions and put things in the chart, but I don’t remember anyone saying I had something like that.\n\nMy husband had all the serious hospital stuff, so sometimes I get mixed up about which doctor said what to who, because there were so many papers and appointments… but for me, no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: When you talk, do people often tell you that you jump from one topic to another in a way they can’t follow?\n\nPatient: Yes… people do say that. Mostly when I’m trying to explain what’s been happening, because it all connects in my head, but when I say it out loud it comes out wrong.\n\nLike I’ll start with my husband’s heart pills, because that’s where I notice things, and then I’m talking about the church schedule, and then the numbers on my client list, and my sister will say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” or “You’re jumping around.” But I’m not trying to confuse anybody. I’m trying to get all the pieces in the right order, and they don’t let me finish.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences began, had you started using any new medication or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so, but then there are always little things—vitamins, something for a headache, whatever someone tells you is good at church or at the store. I try to be careful because after my husband’s hospital stay there were so many bottles around, and I was always checking labels and moving things so they wouldn’t get mixed up.\n\nBut for me, like a new drug or something I started right before all this… I can’t really say there was one. I don’t use street drugs, if that’s what you mean. Medications, I’d have to look at the bottles or the papers because I don’t want to say it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have people noticed that you show much less emotion in your face or voice than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really noticed my face, unless I’m looking in the mirror, and who really does that except when you’re fixing your hair or checking if you look tired before work.\n\nPeople have told me I look tired or serious sometimes, but that could be anything. When you’re cleaning houses all day and then somebody wants to talk and talk, you don’t always have a big expression ready, you know? My sister might say, “Why are you looking like that?” but she says that about everybody.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s less emotion, exactly. I don’t think anyone has said it that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences started, were you diagnosed with any serious medical illness?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had the usual things checked over the years, like when they take your blood pressure at those little clinic visits or when you go in because you’re tired and they want to run tests, but “serious illness”… nobody sat me down and said it like that to me.\n\nBefore all this started, I was mostly working and taking care of the house, and there was always someone else’s appointment or paperwork to worry about. I’d have to look back at the records if you need an exact answer, because I don’t want to mix things up. But as far as I remember, no, I wasn’t diagnosed with something serious right before it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

But as far as I remember, no, I wasn’t diagnosed with something serious right before it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having problems with your memory?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 20\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The doctor is asking about memory problems, but the provided symptom profile does not include memory impairment or cognitive symptoms as an item."}

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Before these experiences began, had you started using any new medication or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so, but then there are always little things—vitamins, something for a headache, whatever someone tells you is good at church or at the store. I try to be careful because after my husband’s hospital stay there were so many bottles around, and I was always checking labels and moving things so they wouldn’t get mixed up.\n\nBut for me, like a new drug or something I started right before all this… I can’t really say there was one. I don’t use street drugs, if that’s what you mean. Medications, I’d have to look at the bottles or the papers because I don’t want to say it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have people noticed that you show much less emotion in your face or voice than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really noticed my face, unless I’m looking in the mirror, and who really does that except when you’re fixing your hair or checking if you look tired before work.\n\nPeople have told me I look tired or serious sometimes, but that could be anything. When you’re cleaning houses all day and then somebody wants to talk and talk, you don’t always have a big expression ready, you know? My sister might say, “Why are you looking like that?” but she says that about everybody.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s less emotion, exactly. I don’t think anyone has said it that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences started, were you diagnosed with any serious medical illness?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had the usual things checked over the years, like when they take your blood pressure at those little clinic visits or when you go in because you’re tired and they want to run tests, but “serious illness”… nobody sat me down and said it like that to me.\n\nBefore all this started, I was mostly working and taking care of the house, and there was always someone else’s appointment or paperwork to worry about. I’d have to look back at the records if you need an exact answer, because I don’t want to mix things up. But as far as I remember, no, I wasn’t diagnosed with something serious right before it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having problems with your memory?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / maid_or_housekeeping_cleaner / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
My memory? I don’t know… I mean, I forget little things sometimes, like where I put my keys or if I already moved the laundry over, but I think everybody does that, especially when you’re tired and going from one house to another and people are asking you things.

Sometimes I’ll walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” but then I remember when I go back, you know? My mother used to say that means you’re doing too many things at once. I haven’t really sat down and thought of it as a problem-problem. What kind of memory do you mean?

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22, F06.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because people keep telling me I’m making too much of things, but I’m not. I know when I’m being watched. The hospital people, and some people from the agency, they’ve been watching our house and even my cleaning routes, like checking up on me to see if I’m taking proper care of my husband.\n\nAnd I do take care of him, you know. I’ve always been a hard worker, I clean the way my mother taught me, corners and baseboards, not just the middle where people look. But lately it feels like everywhere I go, somebody is looking, like they’re keeping notes. That’s why I came in, because everyone acts like I’m imagining it, and I need someone to understand what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, sometimes. Not all the time. When the house is quiet, especially by the kitchen, I’ll hear a woman’s voice near the window, clear like she’s right there. She tells me to check the locks, or move his pills.\n\nAnd I do check the locks anyway, because you can’t be too careful—my mother always said a house should be kept proper, doors, windows, everything in its place. But the voice, it’s not like a thought exactly. It sounds outside of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any major medical illness or head injury before these experiences started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not that I can point to. I’ve had the usual aches from cleaning houses, you know, knees and back, carrying buckets and bending all day, but a major illness or hitting my head bad before all this? I don’t think so.\n\nUnless you mean something like when you get worn down and tired—because people don’t count that, but it does something to you. My mother used to say you can scrub a floor until it shines and still nobody sees what it costs you. But no, I can’t think of a head injury or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sister says that sometimes. She says, “Slow down, I don’t know what you’re talking about.” But I am talking about the same thing, it’s just all connected.\n\nLike when I try to explain about my husband’s heart pills, then I remember the church schedule because that’s when someone could come by, and then the numbers on my client list matter too, because the order of them—well, people act like I skipped something. They say they can’t follow me, but I know what I mean. It just comes out tangled, I guess.\n\nDoctor: For longer than one month, have you been having these experiences of being watched or hearing the woman’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than a month. It’s been, I’d say, around eleven months now with the woman’s voice, and that feeling like people are watching us.\n\nIt started kind of small, like you think maybe it’s just the neighbors or the curtains not sitting right—my mother was always fussy about curtains, said people could see in if you didn’t hang them proper—but no, it’s been nearly a year. Not every minute, but on and off for about eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you been less able to manage your usual work or daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be the one people called when a house was a mess and they needed it done right. But the agency, they took away my supervisory routes. They said clients were complaining, and some of the girls I worked with said they couldn’t count on me the same way anymore.\n\nAnd at home it’s been worse too. My husband needs things kept in order, and I always kept the house running, you know—laundry, meals, appointments, all the little things nobody sees. But lately my husband and my pastor have had to step in more, because I’m not keeping up safely like I used to. It makes me feel ashamed, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you become much less interested in being with other people or doing things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I still care about people… it’s not like I stopped caring. Sometimes I don’t feel like being around a lot of chatter, but then again, some people talk over you and don’t listen anyway.\n\nI don’t know if that’s “less interested” or just me being tired of explaining myself. What do you mean by things I used to care about?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your emotions feel flatter than they used to—for example, that your face shows less expression or your feelings seem muted?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… flatter, like my face? I haven’t really been looking at myself that way. My husband might say I look tired, but he says that when I don’t put my lipstick on too, and honestly lipstick never sits right after a long cleaning shift anyway.\n\nI still feel things, I think. I get upset, I get embarrassed… sometimes I laugh if something’s actually funny. But muted? I’m not sure. Maybe I’m just worn down. What do you mean by “flatter,” like not crying, or just not showing it on my face?\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have people told you that you seem less expressive in your face or voice than before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it exactly like that… “less expressive.” My husband will say, “What’s that look for?” even when I’m not trying to have any look, but he says things like that when he’s irritated too.\n\nAnd my pastor, he’s more gentle, he’ll ask if I’m tired or if I need to sit a minute. But I don’t know if that means my face is different. My voice… I mean, after cleaning all day, anybody’s voice gets low, especially if you’ve been around bleach and dust. I had one client once who kept all these little glass birds on shelves, and dusting around them made me so nervous because if one broke—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. Nobody has really told me plainly that my face or voice is less expressive.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it much harder to get yourself started on everyday tasks, even when you want to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Some days I do drag my feet, but then, doesn’t everybody? If I know the laundry needs doing or the dishes are sitting there, I’ll usually get to it… maybe not right away.\n\nGetting started is funny, because once I’m moving I can keep going, especially with cleaning. At work you don’t really get to sit around wondering if you feel like it—the sink is dirty, the floor needs mopping, you do it. At home it’s different, because the little things pile up, and then I start thinking about which one to do first, and then the phone rings or I remember I need to check if we’re out of trash bags.\n\nSo… harder? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s “much harder.” What kind of tasks do you mean?\n\nDoctor: During the times when you hear the woman’s voice or feel watched, have you also been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day. Sometimes I’m scared or worn out when that happens, and then the whole day feels kind of… heavy, I guess. But I don’t always stop and separate out, like, “Is this sadness, or is this nerves, or am I just tired?”\n\nLike yesterday I was folding towels and I kept thinking I heard something, and then I remembered I had left a rag in the bucket, which is the worst because it gets that sour smell, and then I got upset with myself. So was I depressed? I don’t know. I know I don’t feel right during those times, but “depressed most of the day” is hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your behavior seems strange or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… my husband has said I’m acting “weird,” mostly when I’m going back through the rooms after I already cleaned them. Like I’ll line up the spray bottles under the sink, or the towels have to sit a certain way, and if a chair is angled wrong I’ll fix it and then come back and check it again.\n\nAt work too, sometimes I catch myself arranging things in little patterns—bottles, towels, chairs—and walking room to room again because it just feels wrong to leave it otherwise. People don’t always say it straight out, but I can tell they don’t understand it. And then I start thinking about how some people don’t even rinse the sink after toothpaste, which is—sorry, that’s not what you asked. So yes, I guess they have said it seems strange sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as if someone else is putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Putting thoughts in… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. It’s more that I’ll get a feeling, or I’ll notice something, and then people act like I’m making it up.\n\nThe reason I’m here is because everyone keeps saying I’m imagining things, but I know the hospital people and some people from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. They want to see if I’m taking proper care of my husband. And it’s not like I just decided that out of nowhere—there are little things, like timing, and who shows up, and what they seem to know.\n\nSo no, not exactly putting thoughts in my head. More like they’re watching, and then I can’t stop thinking about what they’re checking.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems begin suddenly, within a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not really within a couple weeks. It’s been… longer than that. I’d say this whole change has been going on about a year and a couple months, since after my husband was in the hospital for his heart.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me trying to keep everything together after that, because when someone comes home from the hospital there’s all these papers and instructions and you’re looking at the floors and the bathroom and thinking everything has to be right. But it didn’t just pass in a week or two. It’s been more like… around a year and two months now.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a neurological illness, such as dementia, Parkinson’s disease, epilepsy, or a stroke?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has told me that, no. I mean, I’ve been to doctors for regular things, and they ask all these questions and put things in the chart, but I don’t remember anyone saying I had something like that.\n\nMy husband had all the serious hospital stuff, so sometimes I get mixed up about which doctor said what to who, because there were so many papers and appointments… but for me, no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: When you talk, do people often tell you that you jump from one topic to another in a way they can’t follow?\n\nPatient: Yes… people do say that. Mostly when I’m trying to explain what’s been happening, because it all connects in my head, but when I say it out loud it comes out wrong.\n\nLike I’ll start with my husband’s heart pills, because that’s where I notice things, and then I’m talking about the church schedule, and then the numbers on my client list, and my sister will say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” or “You’re jumping around.” But I’m not trying to confuse anybody. I’m trying to get all the pieces in the right order, and they don’t let me finish.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences began, had you started using any new medication or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so, but then there are always little things—vitamins, something for a headache, whatever someone tells you is good at church or at the store. I try to be careful because after my husband’s hospital stay there were so many bottles around, and I was always checking labels and moving things so they wouldn’t get mixed up.\n\nBut for me, like a new drug or something I started right before all this… I can’t really say there was one. I don’t use street drugs, if that’s what you mean. Medications, I’d have to look at the bottles or the papers because I don’t want to say it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have people noticed that you show much less emotion in your face or voice than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really noticed my face, unless I’m looking in the mirror, and who really does that except when you’re fixing your hair or checking if you look tired before work.\n\nPeople have told me I look tired or serious sometimes, but that could be anything. When you’re cleaning houses all day and then somebody wants to talk and talk, you don’t always have a big expression ready, you know? My sister might say, “Why are you looking like that?” but she says that about everybody.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s less emotion, exactly. I don’t think anyone has said it that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences started, were you diagnosed with any serious medical illness?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. But I don’t know if that means my face is different. My voice… I mean, after cleaning all day, anybody’s voice gets low, especially if you’ve been around bleach and dust. I had one client once who kept all these little glass birds on shelves, and dusting around them made me so nervous because if one broke—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. Nobody has really told me plainly that my face or voice is less expressive.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it much harder to get yourself started on everyday tasks, even when you want to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Some days I do drag my feet, but then, doesn’t everybody? If I know the laundry needs doing or the dishes are sitting there, I’ll usually get to it… maybe not right away.\n\nGetting started is funny, because once I’m moving I can keep going, especially with cleaning. At work you don’t really get to sit around wondering if you feel like it—the sink is dirty, the floor needs mopping, you do it. At home it’s different, because the little things pile up, and then I start thinking about which one to do first, and then the phone rings or I remember I need to check if we’re out of trash bags.\n\nSo… harder? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s “much harder.” What kind of tasks do you mean?\n\nDoctor: During the times when you hear the woman’s voice or feel watched, have you also been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day. Sometimes I’m scared or worn out when that happens, and then the whole day feels kind of… heavy, I guess. But I don’t always stop and separate out, like, “Is this sadness, or is this nerves, or am I just tired?”\n\nLike yesterday I was folding towels and I kept thinking I heard something, and then I remembered I had left a rag in the bucket, which is the worst because it gets that sour smell, and then I got upset with myself. So was I depressed? I don’t know. I know I don’t feel right during those times, but “depressed most of the day” is hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your behavior seems strange or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… my husband has said I’m acting “weird,” mostly when I’m going back through the rooms after I already cleaned them. Like I’ll line up the spray bottles under the sink, or the towels have to sit a certain way, and if a chair is angled wrong I’ll fix it and then come back and check it again.\n\nAt work too, sometimes I catch myself arranging things in little patterns—bottles, towels, chairs—and walking room to room again because it just feels wrong to leave it otherwise. People don’t always say it straight out, but I can tell they don’t understand it. And then I start thinking about how some people don’t even rinse the sink after toothpaste, which is—sorry, that’s not what you asked. So yes, I guess they have said it seems strange sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as if someone else is putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Putting thoughts in… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. It’s more that I’ll get a feeling, or I’ll notice something, and then people act like I’m making it up.\n\nThe reason I’m here is because everyone keeps saying I’m imagining things, but I know the hospital people and some people from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes. They want to see if I’m taking proper care of my husband. And it’s not like I just decided that out of nowhere—there are little things, like timing, and who shows up, and what they seem to know.\n\nSo no, not exactly putting thoughts in my head. More like they’re watching, and then I can’t stop thinking about what they’re checking.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems begin suddenly, within a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not really within a couple weeks. It’s been… longer than that. I’d say this whole change has been going on about a year and a couple months, since after my husband was in the hospital for his heart.\n\nAt first I thought it was just me trying to keep everything together after that, because when someone comes home from the hospital there’s all these papers and instructions and you’re looking at the floors and the bathroom and thinking everything has to be right. But it didn’t just pass in a week or two. It’s been more like… around a year and two months now.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a neurological illness, such as dementia, Parkinson’s disease, epilepsy, or a stroke?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has told me that, no. I mean, I’ve been to doctors for regular things, and they ask all these questions and put things in the chart, but I don’t remember anyone saying I had something like that.\n\nMy husband had all the serious hospital stuff, so sometimes I get mixed up about which doctor said what to who, because there were so many papers and appointments… but for me, no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: When you talk, do people often tell you that you jump from one topic to another in a way they can’t follow?\n\nPatient: Yes… people do say that. Mostly when I’m trying to explain what’s been happening, because it all connects in my head, but when I say it out loud it comes out wrong.\n\nLike I’ll start with my husband’s heart pills, because that’s where I notice things, and then I’m talking about the church schedule, and then the numbers on my client list, and my sister will say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” or “You’re jumping around.” But I’m not trying to confuse anybody. I’m trying to get all the pieces in the right order, and they don’t let me finish.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences began, had you started using any new medication or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so, but then there are always little things—vitamins, something for a headache, whatever someone tells you is good at church or at the store. I try to be careful because after my husband’s hospital stay there were so many bottles around, and I was always checking labels and moving things so they wouldn’t get mixed up.\n\nBut for me, like a new drug or something I started right before all this… I can’t really say there was one. I don’t use street drugs, if that’s what you mean. Medications, I’d have to look at the bottles or the papers because I don’t want to say it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have people noticed that you show much less emotion in your face or voice than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really noticed my face, unless I’m looking in the mirror, and who really does that except when you’re fixing your hair or checking if you look tired before work.\n\nPeople have told me I look tired or serious sometimes, but that could be anything. When you’re cleaning houses all day and then somebody wants to talk and talk, you don’t always have a big expression ready, you know? My sister might say, “Why are you looking like that?” but she says that about everybody.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s less emotion, exactly. I don’t think anyone has said it that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences started, were you diagnosed with any serious medical illness?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had the usual things checked over the years, like when they take your blood pressure at those little clinic visits or when you go in because you’re tired and they want to run tests, but “serious illness”… nobody sat me down and said it like that to me.\n\nBefore all this started, I was mostly working and taking care of the house, and there was always someone else’s appointment or paperwork to worry about. I’d have to look back at the records if you need an exact answer, because I don’t want to mix things up. But as far as I remember, no, I wasn’t diagnosed with something serious right before it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: My memory? I don’t know… I mean, I forget little things sometimes, like where I put my keys or if I already moved the laundry over, but I think everybody does that, especially when you’re tired and going from one house to another and people are asking you things.\n\nSometimes I’ll walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” but then I remember when I go back, you know? My mother used to say that means you’re doing too many things at once. I haven’t really sat down and thought of it as a problem-problem. What kind of memory do you mean?"
